151	\subsection{Feature Overlap Analysis}
152	\label{app:feature_overlap}
153	
154	We probe whether end-to-end PLM representations capture the same depth signals as our statistical features via linear probing of [CLS] representations from DeBERTa-v3-base and RoBERTa-base.
155	PLM representations excel at surface lexical features (TTR: $R^2{=}0.85$, repeated bigrams) but fail to capture perplexity distribution statistics---\textsc{var\_surprisal} and \textsc{p90\_ppl} yield negative $R^2$ under linear probing, indicating these features are not linearly accessible in the pretrained representation space.
156	This confirms that the 19pp accuracy gap between feature-based and end-to-end methods (\S\ref{app:e2e}) stems from complementary---not redundant---representations: PLMs capture lexical patterns while the targeted statistical features capture distributional tail properties that constitute the dominant depth markers.
157	
158	\subsection{End-to-End Learning}
159	\label{app:e2e}
160	
161	DeBERTa-v3-base \cite{he2021_deberta_v3} and RoBERTa-base \cite{liu2019_roberta} finetuned end-to-end achieve 58.3\% ($\pm$4.7\%) and 59.5\% ($\pm$3.2\%) respectively---lagging the RF baseline (78.2\%) by 19.9pp and 18.7pp.
162	The 1.2pp inter-backbone gap rules out architecture bias; Table~\ref{tab:e2e_detail} shows both backbones share the same failure mode---high d0 F1 ($>$0.94) but collapsed d1/d2 performance (F1 $\leq$ 0.42)---while RF achieves 0.596 d2 recall, a $>$2$\times$ advantage at the hardest depth class.
163	
164	\begin{table}[h]
165	\centering
166	\small
167	\caption{Per-class performance for end-to-end PLM classifiers vs.\ RF baseline (Pythia-1.4B nucleus). PLM columns report F1; RF d2 column reports recall (the class with the largest PLM--RF gap). Both PLM backbones share the same failure pattern: collapsed d1/d2 scores despite adequate d0/d3.}
168	\label{tab:e2e_detail}
169	\begin{tabular}{@{}lccccc@{}}
170	\toprule
171	Method & Acc (\%) & d0 & d1 & d2 & d3 \\
172	\midrule
173	DeBERTa-v3 & 58.3\,$\pm$\,4.7 & .947 & .378 & .247 & .656 \\
174	RoBERTa-base & 59.5\,$\pm$\,3.2 & .958 & .416 & .261 & .647 \\
175	\midrule
176	RF (19 feat.) & 78.2 & --- & --- & .596$^\dagger$ & --- \\
177	\bottomrule
178	\multicolumn{6}{@{}l}{\footnotesize $^\dagger$Recall; shown for the depth class with the largest PLM--RF gap.}
179	\end{tabular}
180	\end{table}
181	
182	\paragraph{Feature space divergence.}
183	SHAP TreeExplainer analysis confirms that perplexity and surprisal features account for 88.8\% of RF's total contribution.
184	Three independent importance measures---SHAP, Gini, and permutation---show strong concordance (Kendall's $W{=}0.90$, $p{<}0.005$; pairwise Spearman $\rho$: SHAP--Gini $0.93$, SHAP--permutation $0.84$; \S\ref{app:permutation}), with \texttt{p90\_ppl} appearing in the SHAP top-2 in 9/9 cells and \texttt{var\_surprisal} in 8/9.
185	Linear probing of PLM [CLS] representations yields negative $R^2$ for both \texttt{var\_surprisal} and \texttt{p90\_ppl} (\S\ref{app:feature_overlap}), indicating that pretrained representations lack the linear structure needed to recover perplexity tail statistics---a systematic representational gap rather than a backbone-specific artifact.
186	
187	DCOL \cite{cloc2025} reached 64.5\% (\S\ref{app:dcol_iterations}); all end-to-end approaches (58--65\%) underperform feature-based methods, suggesting depth estimation benefits from targeted statistical features that capture distributional tail properties absent from PLM representations.
188	This comparison is bounded by backbone scale ($\leq$125M parameters); whether larger PLMs (e.g., 7B+) can close the gap via richer internal representations remains an open question.
189	
190	\subsection{Domain Bias Analysis}
191	\label{app:domain_bias}
192	
193	To assess whether the classifier exploits depth-related features or memorizes stylistic cues from individual seed articles, we conduct three sub-experiments.
194	
195	\paragraph{Methodological note.}
196	An initial implementation assigned sample IDs with a global counter across depths, inadvertently correlating seed-group identity with depth (LOGO accuracy 64.0\%, seed-group prediction 20.8\%).
197	We discovered the issue during internal review, corrected to per-depth seed assignment ensuring each group contains equal samples per depth, and re-ran all sub-experiments.
198	The results below use the corrected pipeline; the initial (buggy) numbers are reported for transparency.
199	
200	\paragraph{Sub-A: Standard CV.}
201	Five-fold CV accuracy on 3-class (d1/d2/d3) classification ranges from 59\% to 82\% across the 9 atlas cells (up to 2.5$\times$ above the 33.3\% chance level), confirming that depth signal is the dominant classification factor.
202	
203	\paragraph{Sub-B: LOGO evaluation.}
204	We assign each passage to one of 10 seed-article groups (balanced: each group contains equal samples per depth) and perform leave-one-group-out CV.
205	LOGO mean accuracy is 72.1\% (range 59.5--81.6\%), matching standard CV (72.0\%) within noise (before fix: 64.0\%, reflecting the depth--group confound).
206	Per-cell fold standard deviations range from 0.4pp to 1.4pp, indicating that cross-group generalization is as reliable as within-group classification.
207	
208	\paragraph{Sub-C: Negative control.}
209	We train two classifiers on identical features: one predicting depth, the other predicting seed-group identity (10-class, chance = 10\%).
210	Depth prediction accuracy averages 72.0\%; seed-group prediction averages 10.2\%---indistinguishable from chance (before fix: 20.8\%, inflated by the depth--group confound).
211	The depth-to-seed-group accuracy ratio is 7.1$\times$, confirming that the feature representation encodes depth but not seed-article identity.
212	
213	Taken together, these results show that seed-article grouping has no measurable effect on depth estimation after correcting the group assignment: LOGO accuracy equals standard CV, and seed-group prediction is at chance.
214

216	\label{app:calibration}
217	
218	Table~\ref{tab:calibration} reports d0 accuracy and false positive rate (FPR) for all three model-specific nucleus RF classifiers on two out-of-distribution human-text corpora: WikiText-103 (546 editorial passages) and C4 (5{,}000 web-crawl passages).
219

228	\midrule

230	 & C4 & 5{,}000 & 79.8 & 20.2 & [19.1, 21.3] \\

240	
241	On WikiText-103, all three classifiers achieve $\geq$98.5\% d0 accuracy, confirming that editorial text is reliably distinguished from recursive generations across model families.
242	On C4, FPR varies sharply: OLMo (4.7\%) vs.\ Pythia (20.2\%) and GPT-2~XL (22.4\%).
243	C4 misclassifications concentrate in template and SEO content with elevated repetition features (\texttt{rep\_3gram} $+$37\%, \texttt{rep\_4gram} $+$75\% vs.\ correctly classified d0 passages), rather than in random noise.
244	The model gap likely reflects training data composition: OLMo (trained on Dolma, which includes diverse web data) produces d0 features more tolerant of web-crawl variation, while Pythia and GPT-2~XL (trained primarily on Wikipedia/WebText) map template-heavy content into the d1+ region.
245	Among C4 false positives for GPT-2~XL, predictions distribute as d1 (9.0\%), d2 (3.7\%), d3 (9.6\%); for OLMo, d1 (2.9\%), d2 (0.5\%), d3 (1.3\%)---the non-uniform depth distribution suggests the classifier maps specific repetition patterns to particular depth signatures rather than producing random misclassifications.
246

312	\subsection{Cross-Scale Full Results}
313	\label{app:cross_scale_full}
314	
315	This section provides the full cross-scale analysis summarized in \S\ref{sec:gen:crossscale}.
316	We extend the benchmark to two 7--8B models: Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct and Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, both under 4-bit NF4 QLoRA fine-tuning, generating depths $d \in \{0, 1, \ldots, 5\}$ with nucleus sampling ($p{=}0.95$, 500 samples per depth).
317	Feature extraction uses the same reference model (Pythia-1.4B) and 19-feature pipeline as all prior experiments.
318	
319	\paragraph{Scale comparison.}
320	Table~\ref{tab:cross_scale} contrasts the two 7--8B models with the three 1--1.5B models on matched evaluation (nucleus sampling, depths 0--3).
321	
322	\begin{table}[h]
323	\centering
324	\small
325	\caption{Cross-scale comparison: 4-class RF accuracy (\%, depths 0--3, nucleus), $\mathrm{EDD}_{T{=}0.70}$ (depths 0--5), and d0-vs-d1 AUC. CI: 95\% bootstrap. Chance = 25\%.}
326	\label{tab:cross_scale}
327	\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}
328	\toprule
329	Model & 4-class acc & $\mathrm{EDD}_{T{=}0.70}$ & d0v1 AUC \\
330	\midrule
331	GPT-2~XL (1.5B) & 83.3 & 4 & 0.999 \\
332	OLMo-1B & 78.8 & 4 & 0.997 \\
333	Pythia-1.4B & 78.2 & 4 & 0.998 \\
334	\midrule
335	Llama-3.1-8B & 70.2\,[68.3, 72.4] & 4 & 0.949 \\

337	\bottomrule
338	\end{tabular}
339	\end{table}
340	
341	\paragraph{(1) Depth signal persists at 7--8B scale.}
342	Both 7--8B models substantially exceed the 25\% chance baseline: Llama-3.1-8B achieves 70.2\% (95\% CI $[68.3, 72.4]$) and Qwen2.5-7B achieves 60.1\% ($[58.0, 62.1]$), confirming that recursive depth remains estimable from perplexity-based features even when the generator is 5--7$\times$ larger than the reference model.
343	Depth-0 separability is preserved in both models: d0v1 AUC is 0.949 (Llama-3.1) and 0.969 (Qwen2.5).
344	
345	\paragraph{(2) EDD at 7--8B scale.}
346	At threshold $T{=}0.70$, Qwen2.5-7B maintains all consecutive-pair AUCs above 0.70 through depth~5 (Table~\ref{tab:cross_scale_auc}), yielding $\mathrm{EDD}_{T{=}0.70} > 5$---exceeding the $\mathrm{EDD}{=}4$ observed at 1--1.5B.
347	Llama-3.1-8B matches the smaller models at $\mathrm{EDD}{=}4$: its monotonic decay causes d4v5 (0.618) to drop below $T$, despite stronger signal at shallower depths.
348	Both larger generators produce weaker per-step signal than the 1--1.5B models (mean consecutive AUC: 0.786 for Llama-3.1, 0.782 for Qwen2.5, vs.\ 0.852 at 1--1.5B).
349	
350	\begin{table}[h]
351	\centering
352	\small
353	\caption{Consecutive binary AUC for 7--8B models (nucleus $p{=}0.95$). Llama-3.1-8B exhibits monotonic decay (d4v5 drops below $T{=}0.70$); Qwen2.5-7B shows a non-monotonic (U-shaped) pattern with all pairs above $T$.}
354	\label{tab:cross_scale_auc}
355	\begin{tabular}{@{}lccccc@{}}
356	\toprule
357	Model & d0v1 & d1v2 & d2v3 & d3v4 & d4v5 \\
358	\midrule
359	Llama-3.1-8B & 0.949 & 0.877 & 0.771 & 0.716 & 0.618 \\
360	Qwen2.5-7B & 0.969 & 0.729 & 0.709 & 0.732 & 0.772 \\
361	\bottomrule
362	\end{tabular}

364	
365	\paragraph{(3) Divergent AUC trajectories and instruction-tuning effect.}
366	Among instruction-tuned models, Llama-3.1-8B exhibits monotonic AUC decay consistent with the 1--1.5B pattern, while Qwen2.5-7B shows a U-shaped recovery beyond d2v3 (Table~\ref{tab:cross_scale_auc}).
367	A base-model control (Llama-3.1-8B without instruction tuning) clarifies the causal direction: the base model achieves 59.9\% four-class accuracy (95\% CI [57.8, 62.15], $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}2$) with non-monotonic AUC decay (Table~\ref{tab:llama3_base_auc}), while the instruction-tuned variant reaches 70.2\% with strictly monotonic decay.
368	The base-model control experiment suggests instruction-tuning regularizes rather than confounds the depth signal.
369	
370	\begin{table}[h]
371	\centering
372	\small
373	\caption{Pairwise AUC: Llama-3.1-8B Base vs.\ Instruct.}
374	\label{tab:llama3_base_auc}
375	\begin{tabular}{lccccc}
376	\toprule
377	Variant & $d_{0\text{v}1}$ & $d_{1\text{v}2}$ & $d_{2\text{v}3}$ & $d_{3\text{v}4}$ & $d_{4\text{v}5}$ \\
378	\midrule
379	Base & 0.918 & 0.794 & 0.682 & 0.730 & 0.595 \\
380	Instruct & 0.965 & 0.874 & 0.771 & 0.716 & 0.618 \\
381	\bottomrule
382	\end{tabular}
383	\end{table}
384	
385	The base model exhibits non-monotonic decay with a U-shaped recovery at $d_{3\text{v}4}$, while the instruction-tuned variant shows strictly monotonic decay. Both variants exceed chance (AUC $> 0.5$) at all boundaries, confirming depth signal exists regardless of instruction-tuning, though instruction-tuning strengthens and regularizes it.
386	
387	\paragraph{Caveats.}
388	For Qwen2.5-7B, the CI lower bound (58.0\%) does not exclude sub-60\% accuracy; Llama-3.1-8B's lower bound (68.3\%) is more comfortably above chance but still below the 75--83\% range at 1--1.5B.
389	The divergent AUC trajectories across two models caution against strong generalizations from only two 7--8B data points: both the monotonic (Llama-3.1) and non-monotonic (Qwen2.5) patterns are single observations, and further architectures are needed to establish which is the dominant regime at this scale.
390	
391	\paragraph{Confounds.}
392	\label{app:cross_scale_confounds}
393	Four confounds limit these results: (1)~4-bit NF4 QLoRA quantization (absent at 1--1.5B) may distort the perplexity distribution independently of depth; (2)~smaller sample size (500 vs.\ 5{,}000 per depth) widens confidence intervals; (3)~evaluation is limited to nucleus sampling, leaving other strategies untested at this scale; (4)~vocabulary mismatch between 7--8B generators and the 1.4B reference model.
394	The base-model control partially addresses a former confound (instruction-tuning): since the base variant shows weaker signal with non-monotonic decay while the instruction-tuned variant shows stronger monotonic signal, instruction-tuning regularizes rather than confounds depth estimation.
395	The remaining confounds are not mutually independent---quantization and vocabulary mismatch both affect the perplexity distribution---making it infeasible to attribute the observed accuracy gap (60--70\% vs.\ 78--85\% at 1--1.5B) to any single factor.
396	
397	\subsection{Cross-Boundary Generalization}
398	\label{app:cross_boundary}
399	
400	The preceding experiments assume a single model family generates all recursive depths.
401	In practice, recursive contamination may traverse model boundaries: text generated by model~A is used to train model~B, whose output trains model~C.
402	We probe whether the depth signal persists under two relaxations of the single-model assumption.
403	
404	\paragraph{Cross-model recursive chains.}
405	We construct a heterogeneous chain: human text (d0) $\to$ Pythia-1.4B generates d1 $\to$ OLMo-1B finetuned on Pythia-d1 generates d2 $\to$ GPT-2~XL finetuned on OLMo-d2 generates d3.
406	Each recursive step uses a \emph{different} model family, eliminating the possibility that depth signal arises from a single model's idiosyncratic behavior.
407	A 4-class Random Forest achieves 84.75\% accuracy (95\% CI $[81.0, 88.25]$), with per-depth F1 scores of 0.98 (d0), 0.88 (d1), 0.76 (d2), and 0.76 (d3).
408	Pairwise AUC remains high: d0-vs-d1 = 0.999 and d2-vs-d3 = 0.841.
409	These results suggest that distributional artifacts of recursive generation persist across model boundaries---providing preliminary evidence consistent with a process-level depth signal rather than individual generator artifacts.
410	
411	\paragraph{Confound discussion.}
412	The heterogeneous chain confounds architecture identity with depth (d1=Pythia, d2=OLMo, d3=GPT-2~XL).
413	However, if the classifier exploited architecture fingerprints rather than depth signal, near-perfect accuracy would be expected since architecture perfectly determines depth label.
414	The observed 84.75\% with monotonically decreasing per-depth F1 (d0=0.98, d1=0.88, d2=0.76, d3=0.76) is more consistent with a depth-signal interpretation, as architecture fingerprinting would not produce this graded pattern.
415	We address this confound directly with a reverse-chain control below.
416	
417	\paragraph{Reverse-chain control.}
418	To empirically test the architecture confound hypothesis, we constructed a reverse chain (GPT-2~XL $\to$ OLMo $\to$ Pythia) reversing the model-depth assignment.
419	The reverse chain achieves 85.75\% (95\% CI [82.0, 89.25]), statistically indistinguishable from the forward chain (84.75\%, [81.0, 88.25]).
420	If the classifier exploited architecture fingerprints rather than depth signal, reversing the chain should invert depth predictions; the equivalent accuracy under both orderings provides empirical evidence against the architecture confound.
421	
422	\paragraph{Cross-domain transfer.}
423	All prior experiments use WikiText-103 as the seed corpus.
424	To test domain dependence, we repeat the pipeline with arXiv abstracts as the seed text (Pythia-1.4B, nucleus $p{=}0.95$, 500 samples per depth, d0--d3).
425	Five-fold cross-validation yields 74.1\% $\pm$ 2.0\% accuracy, compared to ${\sim}$78\% on the WikiText seed.
426	Per-depth F1 shows the familiar pattern: d0 = 1.00, d1 = 0.78, d2 = 0.52, d3 = 0.66---with d2 remaining the most challenging class.
427	The 4pp accuracy gap relative to WikiText likely reflects the narrower stylistic range of scientific abstracts, which may reduce the distributional contrast between adjacent depths.
428	These results provide preliminary evidence that the perplexity-based features capture properties of the recursive generation process rather than domain-specific artifacts of the seed corpus.
429	
430	\subsection{Cross-Seed Replication}
431	\label{app:seed_replication}
432	
433	Table~\ref{tab:seed_replication} reports within-seed and cross-seed classification accuracy for Pythia-1.4B (nucleus, depths 0--3) under two independent LoRA training seeds.
434	
435	\begin{table}[h]
436	\centering
437	\small
438	\caption{Cross-seed transfer (Pythia-1.4B nucleus). Baseline seed=42 is the primary benchmark LoRA; seed=43 is an independent replication. $\Delta$: difference from respective comparison point.}
439	\label{tab:seed_replication}
440	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}
441	\toprule
442	Condition & Acc (\%) & $\Delta$ (pp) \\
443	\midrule
444	Within-seed (seed=42) & 78.20 & --- \\
445	Within-seed (seed=43) & 77.15 & $-$1.05$^\dagger$ \\
446	\midrule
447	Cross-seed (train 42$\to$test 43) & 77.85 & \\
448	Cross-seed (train 43$\to$test 42) & 75.68 & \\
449	Cross-seed avg & 76.76 & $-$1.30$^\ddagger$ \\
450	\bottomrule
451	\multicolumn{3}{@{}l}{\footnotesize $^\dagger$vs.\ baseline (78.20\%). $^\ddagger$vs.\ within-seed avg (78.07\%).}
452	\end{tabular}
453	\end{table}
454	
455	The seed=43 LoRA achieves 77.15\% (95\% CI [76.4, 78.15]), within 1.05\,pp of the seed=42 baseline.
456	Cross-seed transfer averages 76.76\%, a 1.30\,pp deficit relative to within-seed performance.
457	Top-5 feature importance is identical across seeds (\texttt{var\_surprisal}, \texttt{p90\_ppl}, \texttt{mean\_ppl}, \texttt{var\_ppl}, \texttt{p75\_ppl}), confirming that depth signal arises from the recursive process itself rather than idiosyncratic LoRA weight configurations.
458	
459	\subsection{Generation Length Ablation}
460	\label{app:length_ablation}
461	
462	Table~\ref{tab:length_ablation} reports classification accuracy as a function of passage length (Pythia-1.4B nucleus, depths 0--3).
463	
464	\begin{table}[h]
465	\centering
466	\small
467	\caption{Generation length ablation (Pythia-1.4B nucleus, 5-fold CV). Accuracy increases monotonically with passage length; the 9.47\,pp spread confirms that longer passages provide stronger statistical signal.}
468	\label{tab:length_ablation}
469	\begin{tabular}{@{}lc@{}}
470	\toprule
471	Passage length (tokens) & Acc (\%) \\
472	\midrule
473	128 & 68.35 \\
474	256 & 73.80 \\
475	512 & 77.82 \\
476	\bottomrule
477	\end{tabular}
478	\end{table}
479	
480	Depth signal is detectable from 128 tokens (2.7$\times$ chance) and saturates near 500 tokens.
481	The monotonic increase reflects the statistical nature of the features: longer passages yield more stable estimates of perplexity percentiles and surprisal variance, reducing within-class overlap at adjacent depth boundaries.
482	
483	\subsection{Mixed-Depth Corpus}
484	\label{app:mixed_depth}
485	
486	Per-passage classification is largely independent of corpus composition: evaluating on a corpus containing mixed proportions of d0--d3 passages (rather than balanced classes) yields macro-recall $\Delta{=}{-}1.62$\,pp relative to the balanced benchmark (within the 2\,pp tolerance).
487	Individual class deltas range from $-$0.45\,pp to $-$2.43\,pp (d1 shows the largest shift), confirming that the RF classifier operates on per-sample features without sensitivity to the class distribution of the evaluation corpus.
488	
489	\subsection{Extended Discussion}
490	\label{app:extended_discussion}
491	
492	\paragraph{Depth-graduated curation.}
493	Current data curation operates on a binary human-vs-machine distinction \cite{dsir2023,qurating2024}.
494	Recursive generation depth estimation enables a finer-grained alternative: retain low-depth generations (d1) whose distributional properties remain close to the human source, while down-weighting or filtering high-depth text (d3+) where tail erosion has degraded quality.
495	The 70--85\% in-distribution accuracy and $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$ suggest depth-graduated filtering is feasible within known model--strategy configurations, though the d1/d2 boundary inversion under cross-strategy transfer (\S\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy}) means reliable curation requires either known decoding strategy metadata or an upstream strategy-identification step; a pilot study (Appendix~\ref{app:curation_poc}) yields an inconclusive RF-vs-unfiltered comparison ($p>0.4$), though RF-filtered subsets significantly outperform size-matched random subsampling ($p<0.001$--$0.02$); large-scale validation remains open.
496	Importance weighting \cite{sugiyama2007_importance_weighting} could further enable continuous depth-aware data selection rather than discrete thresholding.
497	
498	\paragraph{Toward a provenance toolkit.}
499	Depth estimation complements existing provenance methods along orthogonal axes.
500	Watermarking \cite{kirchenbauer2023_watermark} provides high-confidence attribution when the generator cooperates but is inapplicable to unwatermarked text.
501	Source attribution \cite{uchendu2020_attribution} identifies the generating model but not the generation history.
502	Depth estimation characterizes \emph{iterative reuse} regardless of generator cooperation or identity.
503	A layered provenance system combining these three signals---watermark presence, source model, and recursive generation depth---could support more nuanced data auditing than any single method.
504	
```

the deficit is most pronounced under top-$k$ sampling (46--53\\%), where vocabulary truncation removes the tail tokens that carry depth-discriminative signal.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Per-class recall (\\%) for all 9 atlas cells. d2 is the lowest-recall class in every cell. All experiments use Pythia-1.4B nucleus data. RF baseline: 78.2\\%.}\n\\label{tab:dcol_iterations}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llp{2.2cm}p{2.8cm}@{}}\n\\toprule\nVer. & Backbone & Config & Outcome \\\\\n\\midrule\nv1 & DeBERTa & FT, m\\_lr $10^{-3}$ & Embed.\\ collapse (31.5\\%) \\\\\nv2 & DeBERTa & FT, m\\_lr $10^{-4}$ & OOM \\\\\nv3 & DeBERTa & FT, m\\_lr $10^{-4}$ & Forgetting + OOM \\\\\nv4 & DeBERTa & LoRA, m\\_lr $10^{-3}$ & Embed.\\ collapse \\\\\nv5 & DeBERTa & LoRA, m\\_lr $10^{-4}$ & NaN gradients \\\\\nv6 & RoBERTa & FT, grad clip 1.0 & Stable; 64.5\\% ($-$13.7pp) \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Zero-Shot LLM Prompt Template}\n\\label{app:zeroshot_prompt}\n\nBoth DeepSeek V3.2 and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct use identical prompting.\nThe system message defines recursive generation depth with per-depth descriptions (depth~0: human-written; depth~1--3: successive LLM generation cycles) and lists expected textual patterns at each depth (vocabulary richness, repetition, coherence).\nThe user message presents the passage (truncated to ${\\approx}$384 words) and requests a JSON response containing an integer depth prediction (0--3), a confidence score, and a one-sentence reasoning.\nNo in-context examples are provided.\n\n\\begin{quote}\n\\small\n\\textbf{System:} ``You are an expert at detecting AI-generated text and estimating its generation depth. `Recursive generation depth' refers to how many times a text has been through an LLM generation cycle: Depth~0: Original human-written text [\\ldots] Depth~3: Text generated by an LLM given depth-2 text as input. Key patterns by depth: Depth~0 (human): Rich vocabulary [\\ldots] Depth~3 (third-gen): Noticeable repetition, formulaic structure [\\ldots]''\n\n\\textbf{User:} ``Analyze the following text and estimate its recursive generation depth (0, 1, 2, or 3). TEXT: \\{text\\}. Respond with ONLY a JSON object: \\{\\texttt{\"depth\"}: $\\langle$int 0--3$\\rangle$, \\texttt{\"confidence\"}: $\\langle$float 0--1$\\rangle$, \\texttt{\"reasoning\"}: $\\langle$str$\\rangle$\\}''\n\\end{quote}\n\n\\subsection{Temperature Robustness}\n\\label{app:multi_tau}\n\nThe cross-strategy results in \\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy} use a single temperature ($\\tau{=}0.9$).\nTo test whether depth signal generalizes across temperatures, we repeat the classification pipeline at $\\tau \\in \\{0.5, 0.7, 0.9, 1.1\\}$ on Pythia-1.4B.\n\n\\paragraph{Within-$\\tau$ robustness.}\nFor $\\tau \\in [0.5, 0.9]$, d1-vs-d2 AUC exceeds 0.80 (peak 0.851 at $\\tau{=}0.7$, comparable to nucleus $p{=}0.95$'s 0.861) and 4-class accuracy ranges from 75.4\\% to 77.3\\% (Table~\\ref{tab:multi_tau_within}), confirming the depth signal is not an artifact of a single temperature.\nThe same top-5 features (\\texttt{var\\_surprisal}, \\texttt{p90\\_ppl}, \\texttt{mean\\_ppl}, \\texttt{mean\\_surprisal}, \\texttt{p75\\_ppl}) dominate across all $\\tau$ values with only minor rank-order variation.\n\n\\paragraph{Collapse at $\\tau{=}1.1$: memorization as prerequisite.}\nAt $\\tau{=}1.1$, 4-class accuracy drops to 57.2\\% and d1v2 AUC falls to 0.528.\nTraining loss reveals the mechanism: at low temperature ($\\tau{=}0.5$), fine-tuning loss decreases steeply across recursive depths ($2.27 \\to 0.42 \\to 0.16$), indicating rapid memorization of the model's own output.\nAt $\\tau{=}1.1$, loss remains nearly flat ($2.27 \\to 1.91 \\to 1.85$)---high-entropy output resists memorization, so depth-dependent distributional shifts do not accumulate.\nThis establishes \\emph{memorization as a prerequisite} for detectable depth signal: recursive depth is discriminable only when successive fine-tuning iterations progressively reshape the output distribution.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-$\\tau$ non-transferability.}\nLeave-one-$\\tau$-out transfer yields mean accuracy of 46.7\\% with mean d1v2 AUC of 0.319 (Table~\\ref{tab:multi_tau_cross})---worse than cross-strategy transfer (59.1\\%; \\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy}).\nHoldout $\\tau{=}0.5$ produces inverted d1v2 AUC (0.176), confirming that different temperatures produce surprisal distributions with incompatible shapes.\nThis extends the strategy-specificity finding: even within a single decoding family, the feature space is temperature-specific, reinforcing the need for strategy-conditioned classifiers.\n\n\\paragraph{Ecological validity.}\nOn WikiText-103 (546 editorial passages), all three model-specific RF classifiers achieve $\\geq$98.5\\% d0 accuracy despite $+$621\\% domain shift in mean perplexity---partly an easy case, as WikiText's higher perplexity places it farther from d1--d3 in feature space.\nOn noisier web-crawl data (5{,}000 C4 passages), FPR ranges from 4.7\\% (OLMo) to 22.4\\% (GPT-2~XL), concentrated in template/SEO content with high n-gram repetition (Table~\\ref{tab:calibration}); deployment on heterogeneous web data requires \\\\\n0.7 & 75.4 & 0.851 & 0.997 & 0.720 \\\\\n0.9 & 75.9 & 0.808 & 0.991 & 0.781 \\\\\n1.1 & 57.2 & 0.528 & 0.988 & 0.599 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-$\\tau$ leave-one-out transfer (Pythia-1.4B). Train on three temperatures, test on the held-out one.}\n\\label{tab:multi_tau_cross}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}\n\\toprule\nHoldout & Acc (\\%) & d1v2 AUC \\\\\n\\midrule\n$\\tau{=}0.5$ & 52.2 & 0.176 \\\\\n$\\tau{=}0.7$ & 41.0 & 0.299 \\\\\n$\\tau{=}0.9$ & 46.4 & 0.277 \\\\\n$\\tau{=}1.1$ & 47.1 & 0.525 \\\\\n\\midrule\nMean & 46.7 & 0.319 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Feature Overlap Analysis}\n\\label{app:feature_overlap}\n\nWe probe whether end-to-end PLM representations capture the same depth signals as our statistical features via linear probing of [CLS] representations from DeBERTa-v3-base and RoBERTa-base.\nPLM representations excel at surface lexical features (TTR: $R^2{=}0.85$, repeated bigrams) but fail to capture perplexity distribution statistics---\\textsc{var\\_surprisal} and \\textsc{p90\\_ppl} yield negative $R^2$ under linear probing, indicating these features are not linearly accessible in the pretrained representation space.\nThis confirms that the 19pp accuracy gap between feature-based and end-to-end methods (\\S\\ref{app:e2e}) stems from complementary---not redundant---representations: PLMs capture lexical patterns while the targeted statistical features capture distributional tail properties that constitute the dominant depth markers.\n\n\\subsection{End-to-End Learning}\n\\label{app:e2e}\n\nDeBERTa-v3-base \\cite{he2021_deberta_v3} and RoBERTa-base \\cite{liu2019_roberta} finetuned end-to-end achieve 58.3\\% ($\\pm$4.7\\%) and 59.5\\% ($\\pm$3.2\\%) respectively---lagging the RF baseline (78.2\\%) by 19.9pp and 18.7pp.\nThe 1.2pp inter-backbone gap rules out architecture bias; Table~\\ref{tab:e2e_detail} shows both backbones share the same failure mode---high d0 F1 ($>$0.94) but collapsed d1/d2 performance (F1 $\\leq$ 0.42)---while RF achieves 0.596 d2 recall, a $>$2$\\times$ advantage at the hardest depth class.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Per-class performance for end-to-end PLM classifiers vs.\\ RF baseline (Pythia-1.4B nucleus). PLM columns report F1; RF d2 column reports recall (the class with the largest PLM--RF gap). Both PLM backbones share the same failure pattern: collapsed d1/d2 scores despite adequate d0/d3.}\n\\label{tab:e2e_detail}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nMethod & Acc (\\%) & d0 & d1 & d2 & d3 \\\\\n\\midrule\nDeBERTa-v3 & 58.3\\,$\\pm$\\,4.7 & .947 & .378 & .247 & .656 \\\\\nRoBERTa-base & 59.5\\,$\\pm$\\,3.2 & .958 & .416 & .261 & .647 \\\\\n\\midrule\nRF (19 feat.) & 78.2 & --- & --- & .596$^\\dagger$ & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{@{}l}{\\footnotesize $^\\dagger$Recall; shown for the depth class with the largest PLM--RF gap.}\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Feature space divergence.}\nSHAP TreeExplainer analysis confirms that perplexity and surprisal features account for 88.8\\% of RF's total contribution.\nThree independent importance measures---SHAP, Gini, and permutation---show strong concordance (Kendall's $W{=}0.90$, $p{<}0.005$; pairwise Spearman $\\rho$: SHAP--Gini $0.93$, SHAP--permutation $0.84$; \\S\\ref{app:permutation}), with \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} appearing in the SHAP top-2 in 9/9 cells and \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} in 8/9.\nLinear probing of PLM [CLS] representations yields negative $R^2$ for both \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} and \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} (\\S\\ref{app:feature_overlap}), indicating that pretrained representations lack the linear structure needed to recover perplexity tail statistics---a systematic representational gap rather than a backbone-specific artifact.\n\nDCOL \\cite{cloc2025} reached 64.5\\% (\\S\\ref{app:dcol_iterations}); all end-to-end approaches (58--65\\%) underperform feature-based methods, suggesting depth estimation benefits from targeted statistical features that capture distributional tail properties absent from PLM representations.\nThis comparison is bounded by backbone scale ($\\leq$125M parameters); whether larger PLMs (e.g., 7B+) can close the gap via richer internal representations remains an open question.\n\n\\subsection{Domain Bias Analysis}\n\\label{app:domain_bias}\n\nTo assess whether the classifier exploits depth-related features or memorizes stylistic cues from individual seed articles, we conduct three sub-experiments.\n\n\\paragraph{Methodological note.}\nAn initial implementation assigned sample IDs with a global counter across depths, inadvertently correlating seed-group identity with depth (LOGO accuracy 64.0\\%, seed-group prediction 20.8\\%).\nWe discovered the issue during internal review, corrected to per-depth seed assignment ensuring each group contains equal samples per depth, and re-ran all sub-experiments.\nThe results below use the corrected pipeline; the initial (buggy) numbers are reported for transparency.\n\n\\paragraph{Sub-A: Standard CV.}\nFive-fold CV accuracy on 3-class (d1/d2/d3) classification ranges from 59\\% to 82\\% across the 9 atlas cells (up to 2.5$\\times$ above the 33.3\\% chance level), confirming that depth signal is the dominant classification factor.\n\n\\paragraph{Sub-B: LOGO evaluation.}\nWe assign each passage to one of 10 seed-article groups (balanced: each group contains equal samples per depth) and perform leave-one-group-out CV.\nLOGO mean accuracy is 72.1\\% (range 59.5--81.6\\%), matching standard CV (72.0\\%) within noise (before fix: 64.0\\%, reflecting the depth--group confound).\nPer-cell fold standard deviations range from 0.4pp to 1.4pp, indicating that cross-group generalization is as reliable as within-group classification.\n\n\\paragraph{Sub-C: Negative control.}\nWe train two classifiers on identical features: one predicting depth, the other predicting seed-group identity (10-class, chance = 10\\%).\nDepth prediction accuracy averages 72.0\\%; seed-group prediction averages 10.2\\%---indistinguishable from chance (before fix: 20.8\\%, inflated by the depth--group confound).\nThe depth-to-seed-group accuracy ratio is 7.1$\\times$, confirming that the feature representation encodes depth but not seed-article identity.\n\nTaken together, these results show that seed-article grouping has no measurable effect on depth estimation after correcting the group assignment: LOGO accuracy equals standard CV, and seed-group prediction is at chance.\n\n\\subsection{Wild-Data Calibration}\n\\label{app:calibration}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:calibration} reports d0 accuracy and false positive rate (FPR) for all three model-specific nucleus RF classifiers on two out-of-distribution human-text corpora: WikiText-103 (546 editorial passages) and C4 (5{,}000 web-crawl passages).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Wild-data d0 accuracy and FPR for nucleus RF classifiers. All passages are human-written (true d0). FPR = fraction misclassified as d1+. 95\\% Wilson CIs computed for C4 ($n{=}5{,}000$).}\n\\label{tab:calibration}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llrccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & Source & $n$ & d0 (\\%) & FPR (\\%) & 95\\% Wilson CI \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia-1.4B & WikiText & 546 & 100.0 & 0.0 & --- \\\\\n & C4 & 5{,}000 & 79.8 & 20.2 & [19.1, 21.3] \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nGPT-2~XL & WikiText & 546 & 99.3 & 0.7 & --- \\\\\n & C4 & 5{,}000 & 77.6 & 22.4 & [21.3, 23.6] \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nOLMo-1B & WikiText & 546 & 98.5 & 1.5 & --- \\\\\n & C4 & 5{,}000 & 95.3 & 4.7 & [4.2, 5.3] \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nOn WikiText-103, all three classifiers achieve $\\geq$98.5\\% d0 accuracy, confirming that editorial text is reliably distinguished from recursive generations across model families.\nOn C4, FPR varies sharply: OLMo (4.7\\%) vs.\\ Pythia (20.2\\%) and GPT-2~XL (22.4\\%).\nC4 misclassifications concentrate in template and SEO content with elevated repetition features (\\texttt{rep\\_3gram} $+$37\\%, \\texttt{rep\\_4gram} $+$75\\% vs.\\ correctly classified d0 passages), rather than in random noise.\nThe model gap likely reflects training data composition: OLMo (trained on Dolma, which includes diverse web data) produces d0 features more tolerant of web-crawl variation, while Pythia and GPT-2~XL (trained primarily on Wikipedia/WebText) map template-heavy content into the d1+ region.\nAmong C4 false positives for GPT-2~XL, predictions distribute as d1 (9.0\\%), d2 (3.7\\%), d3 (9.6\\%); for OLMo, d1 (2.9\\%), d2 (0.5\\%), d3 (1.3\\%)---the non-uniform depth distribution suggests the classifier maps specific repetition patterns to particular depth signatures rather than producing random misclassifications.\n\n\\subsection{Downstream Curation PoC}\n\\label{app:curation_poc}\n\nTo validate that depth estimation translates to actionable data curation, we conduct a proof-of-concept downstream evaluation.\nWe fine-tune Pythia-410M with LoRA ($r{=}16$, $\\alpha{=}32$) for 3 epochs on 8 data conditions derived from the Pythia nucleus depth-estimation corpus, repeating each condition over 5 random seeds.\nConditions vary the selection mechanism (RF-predicted depth, oracle depth, random subsampling) and stringency (strict $\\approx$25\\%, moderate $\\approx$50\\%, soft threshold).\nTable~\\ref{tab:curation_poc} reports per-condition perplexity; Table~\\ref{tab:curation_comparisons} reports pairwise contrasts.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Curation PoC: per-condition perplexity (Pythia-410M LoRA, 5 seeds). $\\Delta$ is signed so that positive values indicate lower (better) perplexity than unfiltered. $^*$\\,: $p<0.05$ (paired $t$-test vs.\\ unfiltered).}\n\\label{tab:curation_poc}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nCondition & Mean PPL & Std & $n$ & $\\Delta$ vs Unfilt. & $p$ & $d_z$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nunfiltered        & 15.776 & 0.066 & 10{,}000 & ---      & ---   & ---      \\\\\nrf\\_soft          & 15.733 & 0.038 &  4{,}422 & +0.043   & 0.407 &  0.41    \\\\\nrf\\_moderate      & 15.756 & 0.056 &  4{,}940 & +0.021   & 0.492 &  0.34    \\\\\nrf\\_strict        & 15.810 & 0.061 &  2{,}496 & $-$0.034 & 0.607 & $-$0.25  \\\\\noracle\\_moderate  & 15.809 & 0.103 &  5{,}000 & $-$0.033 & 0.485 & $-$0.34  \\\\\noracle\\_strict    & 15.879 & 0.128 &  2{,}500 & $-$0.103 & 0.216 & $-$0.66  \\\\\nrandom\\_soft      & 16.062 & 0.079 &  4{,}422 & $-$0.286 & ${<}$0.001$^*$ & $-$2.77  \\\\\nrandom\\_moderate  & 15.998 & 0.090 &  4{,}940 & $-$0.222 & 0.028$^*$ & $-$1.50  \\\\\nrandom\\_strict    & 16.239 & 0.165 &  2{,}496 & $-$0.463 & 0.013$^*$ & $-$1.89  \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Pairwise comparisons (paired $t$-test, 5 seeds). $^*$\\,: $p<0.05$; $^{**}$\\,: $p<0.01$.}\n\\label{tab:curation_comparisons}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nComparison & $\\Delta$ PPL & $t$ & $p$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nrf\\_strict vs random\\_strict     & 0.429 & $-$7.05 & 0.002$^{**}$ \\\\\nrf\\_moderate vs random\\_moderate & 0.242 & $-$3.77 & 0.020$^*$    \\\\\nrf\\_soft vs random\\_soft         & 0.328 & $-$17.98 & ${<}$0.001$^{**}$ \\\\\nrf\\_soft vs unfiltered           & 0.043 &    0.93 & 0.407        \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Reference Model Ablation}\n\\label{app:ref_ablation}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Reference model ablation. Classification accuracy (5-fold CV) on Pythia-1.4B generated text (nucleus, d0--d3) using different reference models for feature extraction.}\n\\label{tab:ref_ablation}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\nReference Model & Architecture & Accuracy (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia-1.4B (self) & GPT-NeoX & 78.6 $\\pm$ 0.6 \\\\\nOLMo-1B (cross) & OLMo & 74.5 $\\pm$ 0.7 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Scale Full Results}\n\\label{app:cross_scale_full}\n\nThis section provides the full cross-scale analysis summarized in \\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossscale}.\nWe extend the benchmark to two 7--8B models: Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct and Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, both under 4-bit NF4 QLoRA fine-tuning, generating depths $d \\in \\{0, 1, \\ldots, 5\\}$ with nucleus sampling ($p{=}0.95$, 500 samples per depth).\nFeature extraction uses the same reference model (Pythia-1.4B) and 19-feature pipeline as all prior experiments.\n\n\\paragraph{Scale comparison.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross_scale} contrasts the two 7--8B models with the three 1--1.5B models on matched evaluation (nucleus sampling, depths 0--3).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-scale comparison: 4-class RF accuracy (\\%, depths 0--3, nucleus), $\\mathrm{EDD}_{T{=}0.70}$ (depths 0--5), and d0-vs-d1 AUC. CI: 95\\% bootstrap. Chance = 25\\%.}\n\\label{tab:cross_scale}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & 4-class acc & $\\mathrm{EDD}_{T{=}0.70}$ & d0v1 AUC \\\\\n\\midrule\nGPT-2~XL (1.5B) & 83.3 & 4 & 0.999 \\\\\nOLMo-1B & 78.8 & 4 & 0.997 \\\\\nPythia-1.4B & 78.2 & 4 & 0.998 \\\\\n\\midrule\nLlama-3.1-8B & 70.2\\,[68.3, 72.4] & 4 & 0.949 \\\\\nQwen2.5-7B & 60.1\\,[58.0, 62.1] & ${>}5$ & 0.969 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{(1) Depth signal persists at 7--8B scale.}\nBoth 7--8B models substantially exceed the 25\\% chance baseline: Llama-3.1-8B achieves 70.2\\% (95\\% CI $[68.3, 72.4]$) and Qwen2.5-7B achieves 60.1\\% ($[58.0, 62.1]$), confirming that recursive depth remains estimable from perplexity-based features even when the generator is 5--7$\\times$ larger than the reference model.\nDepth-0 separability is preserved in both models: d0v1 AUC is 0.949 (Llama-3.1) and 0.969 (Qwen2.5).\n\n\\paragraph{(2) EDD at 7--8B scale.}\nAt threshold $T{=}0.70$, Qwen2.5-7B maintains all consecutive-pair AUCs above 0.70 through depth~5 (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_scale_auc}), yielding $\\mathrm{EDD}_{T{=}0.70} > 5$---exceeding the $\\mathrm{EDD}{=}4$ observed at 1--1.5B.\nLlama-3.1-8B matches the smaller models at $\\mathrm{EDD}{=}4$: its monotonic decay causes d4v5 (0.618) to drop below $T$, despite stronger signal at shallower depths.\nBoth larger generators produce weaker per-step signal than the 1--1.5B models (mean consecutive AUC: 0.786 for Llama-3.1, 0.782 for Qwen2.5, vs.\\ 0.852 at 1--1.5B).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Consecutive binary AUC for 7--8B models (nucleus $p{=}0.95$). Llama-3.1-8B exhibits monotonic decay (d4v5 drops below $T{=}0.70$); Qwen2.5-7B shows a non-monotonic (U-shaped) pattern with all pairs above $T$.}\n\\label{tab:cross_scale_auc}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & d0v1 & d1v2 & d2v3 & d3v4 & d4v5 \\\\\n\\midrule\nLlama-3.1-8B & 0.949 & 0.877 & 0.771 & 0.716 & 0.618 \\\\\nQwen2.5-7B & 0.969 & 0.729 & 0.709 & 0.732 & 0.772 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{(3) Divergent AUC trajectories and instruction-tuning effect.}\nAmong instruction-tuned models, Llama-3.1-8B exhibits monotonic AUC decay consistent with the 1--1.5B pattern, while Qwen2.5-7B shows a U-shaped recovery beyond d2v3 (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_scale_auc}).\nA base-model control (Llama-3.1-8B without instruction tuning) clarifies the causal direction: the base model achieves 59.9\\% four-class accuracy (95\\% CI [57.8, 62.15], $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}2$) with non-monotonic AUC decay (Table~\\ref{tab:llama3_base_auc}), while the instruction-tuned variant reaches 70.2\\% with strictly monotonic decay.\nThe base-model control experiment suggests instruction-tuning regularizes rather than confounds the depth signal.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Pairwise AUC: Llama-3.1-8B Base vs.\\ Instruct.}\n\\label{tab:llama3_base_auc}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\nVariant & $d_{0\\text{v}1}$ & $d_{1\\text{v}2}$ & $d_{2\\text{v}3}$ & $d_{3\\text{v}4}$ & $d_{4\\text{v}5}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nBase & 0.918 & 0.794 & 0.682 & 0.730 & 0.595 \\\\\nInstruct & 0.965 & 0.874 & 0.771 & 0.716 & 0.618 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe base model exhibits non-monotonic decay with a U-shaped recovery at $d_{3\\text{v}4}$, while the instruction-tuned variant shows strictly monotonic decay. Both variants exceed chance (AUC $> 0.5$) at all boundaries, confirming depth signal exists regardless of instruction-tuning, though instruction-tuning strengthens and regularizes it.\n\n\\paragraph{Caveats.}\nFor Qwen2.5-7B, the CI lower bound (58.0\\%) does not exclude sub-60\\% accuracy; Llama-3.1-8B's lower bound (68.3\\%) is more comfortably above chance but still below the 75--83\\% range at 1--1.5B.\nThe divergent AUC trajectories across two models caution against strong generalizations from only two 7--8B data points: both the monotonic (Llama-3.1) and non-monotonic (Qwen2.5) patterns are single observations, and further architectures are needed to establish which is the dominant regime at this scale.\n\n\\paragraph{Confounds.}\n\\label{app:cross_scale_confounds}\nFour confounds limit these results: (1)~4-bit NF4 QLoRA quantization (absent at 1--1.5B) may distort the perplexity distribution independently of depth; (2)~smaller sample size (500 vs.\\ 5{,}000 per depth) widens confidence intervals; (3)~evaluation is limited to nucleus sampling, leaving other strategies untested at this scale; (4)~vocabulary mismatch between 7--8B generators and the 1.4B reference model.\nThe base-model control partially addresses a former confound (instruction-tuning): since the base variant shows weaker signal with non-monotonic decay while the instruction-tuned variant shows stronger monotonic signal, instruction-tuning regularizes rather than confounds depth estimation.\nThe remaining confounds are not mutually independent---quantization and vocabulary mismatch both affect the perplexity distribution---making it infeasible to attribute the observed accuracy gap (60--70\\% vs.\\ 78--85\\% at 1--1.5B) to any single factor.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Boundary Generalization}\n\\label{app:cross_boundary}\n\nThe preceding experiments assume a single model family generates all recursive depths.\nIn practice, recursive contamination may traverse model boundaries: text generated by model~A is used to train model~B, whose output trains model~C.\nWe probe whether the depth signal persists under two relaxations of the single-model assumption.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-model recursive chains.}\nWe construct a heterogeneous chain: human text (d0) $\\to$ Pythia-1.4B generates d1 $\\to$ OLMo-1B finetuned on Pythia-d1 generates d2 $\\to$ GPT-2~XL finetuned on OLMo-d2 generates d3.\nEach recursive step uses a \\emph{different} model family, eliminating the possibility that depth signal arises from a single model's idiosyncratic behavior.\nA 4-class Random Forest achieves 84.75\\% accuracy (95\\% CI $[81.0, 88.25]$), with per-depth F1 scores of 0.98 (d0), 0.88 (d1), 0.76 (d2), and 0.76 (d3).\nPairwise AUC remains high: d0-vs-d1 = 0.999 and d2-vs-d3 = 0.841.\nThese results suggest that distributional artifacts of recursive generation persist across model boundaries---providing preliminary evidence consistent with a process-level depth signal rather than individual generator artifacts.\n\n\\paragraph{Confound discussion.}\nThe heterogeneous chain confounds architecture identity with depth (d1=Pythia, d2=OLMo, d3=GPT-2~XL).\nHowever, if the classifier exploited architecture fingerprints rather than depth signal, near-perfect accuracy would be expected since architecture perfectly determines depth label.\nThe observed 84.75\\% with monotonically decreasing per-depth F1 (d0=0.98, d1=0.88, d2=0.76, d3=0.76) is more consistent with a depth-signal interpretation, as architecture fingerprinting would not produce this graded pattern.\nWe address this confound directly with a reverse-chain control below.\n\n\\paragraph{Reverse-chain control.}\nTo empirically test the architecture confound hypothesis, we constructed a reverse chain (GPT-2~XL $\\to$ OLMo $\\to$ Pythia) reversing the model-depth assignment.\nThe reverse chain achieves 85.75\\% (95\\% CI [82.0, 89.25]), statistically indistinguishable from the forward chain (84.75\\%, [81.0, 88.25]).\nIf the classifier exploited architecture fingerprints rather than depth signal, reversing the chain should invert depth predictions; the equivalent accuracy under both orderings provides empirical evidence against the architecture confound.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-domain transfer.}\nAll prior experiments use WikiText-103 as the seed corpus.\nTo test domain dependence, we repeat the pipeline with arXiv abstracts as the seed text (Pythia-1.4B, nucleus $p{=}0.95$, 500 samples per depth, d0--d3).\nFive-fold cross-validation yields 74.1\\% $\\pm$ 2.0\\% accuracy, compared to ${\\sim}$78\\% on the WikiText seed.\nPer-depth F1 shows the familiar pattern: d0 = 1.00, d1 = 0.78, d2 = 0.52, d3 = 0.66---with d2 remaining the most challenging class.\nThe 4pp accuracy gap relative to WikiText likely reflects the narrower stylistic range of scientific abstracts, which may reduce the distributional contrast between adjacent depths.\nThese results provide preliminary evidence that the perplexity-based features capture properties of the recursive generation process rather than domain-specific artifacts of the seed corpus.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Seed Replication}\n\\label{app:seed_replication}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:seed_replication} reports within-seed and cross-seed classification accuracy for Pythia-1.4B (nucleus, depths 0--3) under two independent LoRA training seeds.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-seed transfer (Pythia-1.4B nucleus). Baseline seed=42 is the primary benchmark LoRA; seed=43 is an independent replication. $\\Delta$: difference from respective comparison point.}\n\\label{tab:seed_replication}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}\n\\toprule\nCondition & Acc (\\%) & $\\Delta$ (pp) \\\\\n\\midrule\nWithin-seed (seed=42) & 78.20 & --- \\\\\nWithin-seed (seed=43) & 77.15 & $-$1.05$^\\dagger$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nCross-seed (train 42$\\to$test 43) & 77.85 & \\\\\nCross-seed (train 43$\\to$test 42) & 75.68 & \\\\\nCross-seed avg & 76.76 & $-$1.30$^\\ddagger$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{@{}l}{\\footnotesize $^\\dagger$vs.\\ baseline (78.20\\%). $^\\ddagger$vs.\\ within-seed avg (78.07\\%).}\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe seed=43 LoRA achieves 77.15\\% (95\\% CI [76.4, 78.15]), within 1.05\\,pp of the seed=42 baseline.\nCross-seed transfer averages 76.76\\%, a 1.30\\,pp deficit relative to within-seed performance.\nTop-5 feature importance is identical across seeds (\\texttt{var\\_surprisal}, \\texttt{p90\\_ppl}, \\texttt{mean\\_ppl}, \\texttt{var\\_ppl}, \\texttt{p75\\_ppl}), confirming that depth signal arises from the recursive process itself rather than idiosyncratic LoRA weight configurations.\n\n\\subsection{Generation Length Ablation}\n\\label{app:length_ablation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:length_ablation} reports classification accuracy as a function of passage length (Pythia-1.4B nucleus, depths 0--3).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Generation length ablation (Pythia-1.4B nucleus, 5-fold CV). Accuracy increases monotonically with passage length; the 9.47\\,pp spread confirms that longer passages provide stronger statistical signal.}\n\\label{tab:length_ablation}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lc@{}}\n\\toprule\nPassage length (tokens) & Acc (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\n128 & 68.35 \\\\\n256 & 73.80 \\\\\n512 & 77.82 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nDepth signal is detectable from 128 tokens (2.7$\\times$ chance) and saturates near 500 tokens.\nThe monotonic increase reflects the statistical nature of the features: longer passages yield more stable estimates of perplexity percentiles and surprisal variance, reducing within-class overlap at adjacent depth boundaries.\n\n\\subsection{Mixed-Depth Corpus}\n\\label{app:mixed_depth}\n\nPer-passage classification is largely independent of corpus composition: evaluating on a corpus containing mixed proportions of d0--d3 passages (rather than balanced classes) yields macro-recall $\\Delta{=}{-}1.62$\\,pp relative to the balanced benchmark (within the 2\\,pp tolerance).\nIndividual class deltas range from $-$0.45\\,pp to $-$2.43\\,pp (d1 shows the largest shift), confirming that the RF classifier operates on per-sample features without sensitivity to the class distribution of the evaluation corpus.\n\n\\subsection{Extended Discussion}\n\\label{app:extended_discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Depth-graduated curation.}\nCurrent data curation operates on a binary human-vs-machine distinction \\cite{dsir2023,qurating2024}.\nRecursive generation depth estimation enables a finer-grained alternative: retain low-depth generations (d1) whose distributional properties remain close to the human source, while down-weighting or filtering high-depth text (d3+) where tail erosion has degraded quality.\nThe 70--85\\% in-distribution accuracy and $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$ suggest depth-graduated filtering is feasible within known model--strategy configurations, though the d1/d2 boundary inversion under cross-strategy transfer (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy}) means reliable curation requires either known decoding strategy metadata or an upstream strategy-identification step; a pilot study (Appendix~\\ref{app:curation_poc}) yields an inconclusive RF-vs-unfiltered comparison ($p>0.4$), though RF-filtered subsets significantly outperform size-matched random subsampling ($p<0.001$--$0.02$); large-scale validation remains open.\nImportance weighting \\cite{sugiyama2007_importance_weighting} could further enable continuous depth-aware data selection rather than discrete thresholding.\n\n\\paragraph{Toward a provenance toolkit.}\nDepth estimation complements existing provenance methods along orthogonal axes.\nWatermarking \\cite{kirchenbauer2023_watermark} provides high-confidence attribution when the generator cooperates but is inapplicable to unwatermarked text.\nSource attribution \\cite{uchendu2020_attribution} identifies the generating model but not the generation history.\nDepth estimation characterizes \\emph{iterative reuse} regardless of generator cooperation or identity.\nA layered provenance system combining these three signals---watermark presence, source model, and recursive generation depth---could support more nuanced data auditing than any single method.\n",

I found comprehensive information about the ACL Rolling Review form and scoring scales for 2025. Here are the key details:

## Scoring System Overview

Starting with February 2025, ARR reviewers are asked to provide three scores: soundness, excitement, and overall recommendation.

## Soundness Scoring Scale (1-5)

The soundness scale includes: 5 = Excellent (this study is one of the most thorough), 4 = Strong (provides sufficient support for all claims), and 3 = Acceptable (provides sufficient support for main claims). The soundness scores must be justified by the text of the review.

## Excitement Scoring Scale (1-5)

The excitement scale includes: 5 = Highly Exciting (would recommend to others and/or attend presentation), 4 = Exciting (would mention to others and/or attend presentation), and 1 = Not Exciting (paper does not resonate and wouldn't with the *ACL community).

Excitement is subjective and doesn't necessarily follow what's popular; reviewers may perceive papers as transformational/innovative/surprising due to conceptual breakthroughs or evidence challenging assumptions, or be excited about potential impact on communities through lowering barriers or enabling new applications.

## Important Notes

Excitement scores reflect personal taste and don't necessarily come with explicit reasons. All papers recommended for Findings and main conferences are expected to be sufficiently sound and reproducible, but a paper can be worthy of the main conference even if the reviewer is not personally excited about it.


REMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks.

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I have all the paper content. Let me also fetch the ARR review form page to get the exact scoring rubric, and check the specific issues mentioned in the task.

Let me now review the paper systematically against the historical issues. Let me trace through each one:

**R1-N2**: Table 2/4/5 仍无 std/CI/significance test。Figure 4 有 bootstrap CI 说明有计算能力但未推广。

Looking at the tables:
- Table 2 (tab:main_matrix): Reports accuracy percentages without std/CI


- Table 4 (tab:edd_sensitivity): Just counts of cells, no variance measures needed
- Table 5 (tab:baselines): Reports accuracy without std/CI
- Figure 4 (fig:auc_decay): Has "Shaded bands: bootstrap 95% CIs"

So the paper does have the capability to compute confidence intervals as shown in Figure 4, but hasn't applied this to the main results tables. Let me verify the exact table numbering in the document to make sure I'm mapping them correctly.

Looking at the main tables, Table 2 and Table 5 both lack standard deviations or confidence intervals. Table 5 does include a $\Delta_{\max}$ column that shows per-cell variation is at most 0.4 percentage points, which gives some sense of spread but isn't a formal uncertainty measure. The appendix does better—the cross-scale results include 95% CIs, the seed replication study reports 95% CIs, and the curation proof-of-concept table shows standard deviations with p-values. So this concern is only partially addressed in the main text, rating it as C.

Now looking at the second reviewer's point about d0 inflation—CIs are used inconsistently across tables like the cross-scale and EDD figure, but not systematically throughout all performance tables. The d0 inflation issue itself appears to be addressed: Table 2 now includes d1-d3 three-class accuracy in parentheses, and the main text explicitly discusses this in the Limitations section, noting that depth 0 achieves over 98% recall across all settings, which inflates the overall accuracy numbers. The cross-model transfer table (Table 6) is also relevant here.

For the d2 recall difficulty, the text states that d2 consistently shows the lowest recall at 46-73% across different cells, and the abstract mentions d1-d3 balanced accuracy at 55.4%, which is 1.7× chance level. So this concern seems resolved.

The fourth reviewer's note about the EDD₀.₇₅=4 statistic in the abstract is more problematic—the significance only holds for Pythia at p=0.001, while OLMo is marginal at p=0.091 and GPT-2 XL is non-significant at p=0.505. The phrase "consistent direction" in the abstract may be overstating the evidence given these mixed results. ), $p = 0.091$ for OLMo (marginal), and $p = 0.505$ for GPT-2 XL (non-significant). So the pattern holds across all three model families, but the statistical evidence weakens considerably—Pythia shows strong significance while the other two are much less convincing. The abstract's phrasing of "consistent direction across all three families with graded statistical support" does capture this stratification, though it's a somewhat gentle way of describing how GPT-2 XL essentially shows no significant effect. Still, by explicitly noting that the strongest claim applies specifically to Pythia, the abstract appropriately constrains the scope of what's being claimed.

For the Related Work section, I'm noticing some key citations are missing. Schaeffer et al. 2025 directly challenges benchmark ecological validity but isn't cited. The ordinal classification foundations—McCullagh 1980 and Rennie & Srebro 2005—aren't referenced either, even though the paper discusses ordinal regression methods. CCNet from Wenzek et al. 2020 is also absent. I should verify this against the references file to be sure, but from what I can see in the Related Work text, these gaps appear to be unaddressed.

For the LoRA rank sensitivity analysis, the method section only specifies r=16 with α=32, and the cross-seed replication uses the same configuration. There's no exploration of how performance varies across different ranks like {4, 16, 64}, so this ablation study is missing.

On the reference model circularity issue, the Limitations section does acknowledge that Pythia serves as both generator and reference, creating a potential dependency problem. The paper suggests adding an architecturally distinct reference model as an ablation, but this hasn't been implemented yet. The single LoRA instance concern is actually addressed—they ran independent replications with different seeds and showed the depth signal persists across them, confirming it's not an artifact of specific weights. For the EDD classifier-dependence issue, the definition explicitly specifies the Random Forest configuration, but there's no explicit statement acknowledging that EDD is fundamentally an operational measure tied to that classifier choice—it's more implicit in how they present it.

Looking at their comparison across different classifiers in Table 5, the spread is minimal (≤0.4pp), which indirectly suggests EDD isn't highly sensitive to classifier choice, but they never directly state this as a limitation or design decision. So this is partially addressed through empirical evidence rather than explicit methodological declaration.

The accumulation scenario—where a training corpus mixes different recursion depths (like 70% d0 and 30% d1)—isn't tested anywhere in the paper. They discuss mixed-depth evaluation sets in the appendix, but that's about class imbalance during testing, not about the corpus itself being a mixture of generations. This gap remains unaddressed.

Now considering whether depth estimation is just quality estimation in disguise. The paper would need matched-quality controls—taking texts of similar quality at different depths and showing they're still distinguishable. The temperature robustness analysis hints at this (the signal collapses when memorization doesn't occur), and the cross-model chains suggest it's about the generation process rather than a single model's quality gradient. But there's no explicit matched-quality control experiment, so while there's indirect evidence, it's not directly addressed.

Looking at presentation issues now: the abstract is quite dense at 10 lines with many numbers packed in. There's also a minor inconsistency where the abstract claims "70–85% four-class accuracy" but Table 2 shows 69.6% to 85.4%—the lower bound should either be stated as 69.6% or rounded consistently. And the EDD_{0.75}=4 claim in the abstract doesn't quite match what I'm seeing in the depth 0-3 grid at that temperature.

The cross-model transfer results are honestly reported with moderate balanced accuracy of 55.4% (1.7× chance baseline), and the curation proof-of-concept shows that RF-filtered versus unfiltered results aren't statistically significant. However, the main tables lack error bars or confidence intervals—only some appendix tables include them, which is a notable gap for a methods paper.

I'm also noticing some methodological concerns: the paper uses the same reference model (Pythia-1.4B) for both feature extraction and as a generator, though the Limitations section does address this by showing GPT-2 XL achieves higher accuracy despite being cross-model. The BOS-seeded generation without prompt conditioning is acknowledged as potentially amplifying the depth signal, but it limits real-world applicability. The 512-token passage length is also quite long for practical use—the 128-token result shows significant performance degradation to 68.35%.

Now I should verify the references before writing up my full review.

Based on the search results, I found the ARR soundness score rubric definitions. Here are the specific criteria for each score level:

## ARR 2025 Soundness Score Rubric

**5 = Excellent:** "This study is one of the most thorough I have seen, given its type."

**4 = Strong:** "This study provides sufficient support for all of its claims. Some extra experiments could be nice, but not essential."

**3 = Acceptable:** "This study provides sufficient support for its main claims."

The search results show the definitions for scores 5, 4, and 3, but the complete definitions for scores 2 and 1 appear to be cut off in the available excerpts. However, based on the context from the review form, score 2 relates to papers that "need substantial revisions that can be completed by the next ARR cycle," and score 1 indicates "This paper has to be fully redone, or it is not relevant to the *ACL community."

The soundness evaluation asks whether the paper is "sufficiently sound and thorough" and considers "the depth and/or breadth of the research questions investigated, technical soundness of experiments, methodological validity of evaluation."

实际范围为 69.6%--85.4%。下界 69.6% 被向上取整至 70%。虽然差异仅 0.4pp，但在 blind review 中审稿人可能质疑数据 cherry-picking。\n- 建议：改为 \"approximately 70--85%\" 或 \"69.6--85.4%\"。\n\n**N2** [critical] Curation PoC 与 narrative 不一致\n- 位置：§6 Discussion, Appendix §A.6\n- 问题：Discussion 将 depth-graduated curation 作为核心 motivation 之一，但 Appendix Table 9 显示 RF-filtered vs. unfiltered 无统计显著差异（p=0.407, 0.492, 0.607）。仅 RF vs. random subsampling 显著（p<0.001--0.02）。这意味着 depth-aware filtering 相比 simply keeping all data 无可证明的益处——只是优于随机丢弃数据。\n- 为什么影响接收：核心 application narrative（\"enables depth-graduated data curation\"）缺乏实验支撑。如果 curation 是 motivation 之一，inconclusive 结果需要在主文而非仅附录中诚实呈现。\n- 建议：(1) 在 Discussion 中明确说明 curation PoC 目前 inconclusive，或 (2) 降低 narrative 中对 curation 的强调，将其定位为 future work 而非 established benefit。\n\n### Should-Fix（影响评分）\n\n**W1** [warning] EDD 在 GPT-2 XL 上的 boundary case\n- 位置：§4.4, Abstract\n- 问题：GPT-2 XL d3-vs-d4 AUC = 0.750，恰好等于 threshold T=0.75。定义上 EDD≥4（因为 ≥），但 bootstrap test p=0.505 说明这个点估计完全无法区分于 random fluctuation。论文将此纳入 \"consistent direction\" 表述中，但数学上 AUC=0.750 at T=0.75 with p=0.505 是一个 null result。\n- 建议：在 §4.4 中明确指出 GPT-2 XL 的 EDD=4 结论在统计上是 trivial 的（AUC 恰好等于阈值且无显著性），避免读者误解三个模型都 robustly 支持 EDD=4。\n\n**W2** [warning] Cross-strategy transfer failure 与 \"universal fingerprint\" narrative 的张力\n- 位置：§5.3, §1（Introduction \"universal and scale-variant\"）\n- 问题：Introduction 声称 fingerprints 是 \"universal and scale-variant\"，但 cross-strategy transfer（§5.3）仅 59.1% mean accuracy，d1-d3 仅 45.9%（chance=33%，1.38×），temperature holdout d1v2 AUC 可低至 0.129（GPT-2 XL）。这不是 \"universal\" 的表现——strategy-conditioned classifiers 是必需的。\n- 建议：将 Introduction 的 \"universal\" 修饰为 \"universal within-strategy\" 或限定 universality 的范围。当前措辞对读者产生过度乐观的印象。\n\n**W3** [warning] 95% CI 未报告在 primary result tables\n- 位置：Table 2, Table 5\n- 问题：5-fold CV 本身产生 5 个 fold-level accuracy，可直接报告 mean±std。Table 2 和 Table 5 是论文最核心的 result tables，却是唯二没有 variance measure 的主要表格。Figure 4 有 bootstrap CI，cross-scale table 有 95% CI——说明作者有计算能力但选择性地省略了。\n- 建议：Table 2 加 ±std 列（或 95% CI），Table 5 的 Δ_max 列改为标准 std 报告。\n\n### Minor（不影响接收）\n\n- Abstract 过度压缩（~250 words，数字密集），建议删减 cross-model chain 的具体百分比（84.75%/85.75%）以提高可读性。\n- §3.4 EDD 定义的 footnote 1 解释了 non-monotonic 情况，但正文未在 Definition 后 explicitly state \"EDD is defined relative to the classifier and feature set specified above\"。\n- Table 2 caption 说 \"Depth-0 recall exceeds 98% in all cells\" 但 Table 8 (Appendix) 显示 GPT-2 XL top-k 为 98.5%——consistent，只是 \"exceeds 98%\" 与 98.5% 的 margin 很小。\n- §1 \"our key insight is that recursive generation leaves quantifiable statistical fingerprints... universal and scale-variant\"：前半句是 post-hoc conclusion 而非 a priori insight；建议改为 hypothesis-driven 表述。\n\n---\n\n## 主要优势\n\n1. **问题新颖性**：Ordinal depth estimation 确实是文献空白。从 binary detection 到 ordinal classification 的推进有明确的科学价值。\n2. **系统性实验设计**：3×3 atlas 设计优雅，覆盖了 model family 和 decoding strategy 两个维度的交叉效应。\n3. **诚实的 Limitations**：8 段 Limitations 覆盖了大部分可预见的批评，这在 submission 中罕见。Cross-seed replication、domain bias analysis、base-model control 都体现了方法学严谨性。\n4. **特征可解释性**：p90_ppl + var_surprisal 作为 dominant features 的一致性（9/9 + 8/9，三种 importance measures concordance W=0.90）令人信服。\n5. **Ablation 充分性**：feature ablation、classifier convergence、passage length、reference model、cross-seed——覆盖面广。\n\n---\n\n## 总结判断\n\n论文定义了一个有价值的新任务，实验覆盖系统性好，但三个核心方法学问题阻碍接收：(1) 主表格缺 CI 降低了 reproducibility claim 的可信度；(2) EDD=4 的跨模型泛化统计支撑不均匀且 abstract 表述 euphemistic；(3) accumulation 场景和 matched-quality control 两个关键生态效度实验完全缺失。这些问题中 (1)(2) 可在一个 cycle 内修复，(3) 需要额外实验但技术上可行。建议 resubmit next cycle。\n\n**Confidence**: 4/5 (I am confident but not certain about my assessment)",

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
The Director is asking me to respond to Reviewer 2's review point by point. I need to evaluate each issue based on what I've read in the paper and give my independent assessment as Reviewer 1, either agreeing or rebutting with evidence from the paper.

Let me go through each one:

**N1** - Schaeffer et al. 2025 missing from Related Work
I already flagged this in my own review (as part of R5-W4). I agree this is a gap.

**N2** - Core finding is just empirical validation of Dohmatob 2024


Dohmatob's work establishes that recursive training erodes distributional tails, and the paper's finding that perplexity tail statistics are the best depth discriminators does align with that theory. But the paper actually contributes several things beyond validation: it frames depth estimation as an ordinal classification problem, introduces the EDD metric, identifies strategy-dependent trajectories that Dohmatob doesn't predict, demonstrates cross-model transfer capabilities, and constructs a novel benchmark. So while there's theoretical overlap, the empirical contributions and practical applications extend meaningfully beyond just confirming existing theory.

Now looking at the abstract itself—it's packed with references like §4.4 and an overwhelming number of metrics and results. The abstract is trying to cram in too much numerical detail, which dilutes the core message.

The universality claims are also problematic. Cross-strategy transfer only reaches 59.1% and cross-τ transfer is even lower at 46.7%, yet the discussion frames these results as confirming "signal universality." That's a significant overstatement given the actual transfer performance.

Looking at the contribution bullet in the introduction, it's packed with too many numbers and claims—cross-model chains, cross-scale observations, cross-domain transfer, temperature robustness ranges, and reference model independence all crammed together. The ablation results are also mentioned but cut off mid-sentence, making the whole bullet feel incomplete and overwhelming.

I'm also noting that CCNet and McCullagh 1980 are both missing from the paper, which I've already flagged in my review.
```

60.1%, 59.9%, 70.2%, 98.5%... 超过 20 个数字。这远超正常 NLP 论文 abstract 密度。\n3. 审稿人在 30 秒内无法从这种密度中提取核心 message。\n\n但我将此标为 should-fix 而非 critical——这是写作质量问题而非方法学缺陷，不应单独影响接收决定。技术内容有效前提下，abstract 可以在 camera-ready 时修订。\n\n---\n\n### Should-Fix 逐条回应\n\n**[AGREE] R2-W1: \"universal and scale-variant\" 矛盾措辞**\n\n完全同意。我在独立审稿中也标记了此问题（我的 W2）。Introduction §1 写 fingerprints are \"universal and scale-variant\"，但 cross-strategy transfer 仅 59.1%（d1-d3: 45.9%, 1.38× chance），temperature holdout d1v2 AUC 低至 0.129 (§5.3 GPT-2 XL)。Strategy-conditioned classifiers 是必需的——这不是 \"universal\" 的定义。\n\n---\n\n**[AGREE] R2-W2: Discussion \"enables\" vs PoC \"inconclusive\" 张力**\n\n完全同意。我在独立审稿中将此标为 N2 (critical)。Discussion §6 写 \"Depth estimation enables depth-graduated data curation\"，但 Appendix Table 9 显示 RF-filtered vs. unfiltered p=0.407/0.492/0.607——全部 non-significant。仅 RF vs. random subsampling 显著。论文主文不应用 \"enables\" 描述一个 inconclusive 的 PoC。\n\n---\n\n**[AGREE] R2-W3: \"signal universality\" overclaim**\n\n同意。generalization.tex 最后一句 (line 110): \"Cross-model recursive chains (84.75%)... further confirm signal universality.\" 但：\n- Cross-strategy transfer 仅 59.1%（§5.3）\n- Cross-τ transfer 仅 46.7%（Appendix §A.4）\n- Cross-model transfer d2 recall 低至 26.8%（Table 6, Pythia holdout）\n\n\"Signal universality\" 在这些数据下是 overclaim。更准确的描述是 \"signal persists across model boundaries under matched strategy conditions\"。\n\n---\n\n**[AGREE] R2-W4: Contribution bullet 3 信息过载**\n\n同意。Introduction contribution list 第三条（introduction.tex lines 28-29）在一个 bullet 中堆砌了 7 个数字和 5 个 generalization axes，读起来像 results summary 而非 contribution statement。Contribution bullets 应陈述 what was achieved，具体数字放在相应 section。\n\n---\n\n**[AGREE] R2-W5: 遗漏 CCNet (Wenzek et al. 2020)**\n\n同意。我在独立审稿中也标记了此遗漏。CCNet 是 perplexity-based web data filtering 的 foundational work，与本文使用 perplexity features 进行 depth-based filtering 有直接方法论关联。Appendix §A.5 讨论 C4 calibration 时不引 CCNet 是明显缺失。\n\n---\n\n**[AGREE] R2-W6: Ordinal classification 引用基础薄弱**\n\n同意。我在独立审稿中也标记了此点。McCullagh 1980 (proportional odds model) 是 ordinal regression 的奠基论文。当前 §2 Ordinal Classification 段仅引 CORAL (2020) 和 CLOC (2025)，缺少理论基础。补充 McCullagh 1980 和 Rennie & Srebro 2005 不需要额外实验，仅需 1-2 句话和 bib entries。\n\n---\n\n### Minor 逐条回应\n\n**[AGREE] em-dash 和 -ing phrases**: Abstract line 1 已有 \"arises---yet\"，虽然技术上是 LaTeX 的三连 hyphen 生成的 em-dash，但仍是 AI 写作的典型标志。同意需要全文清理。\n\n**[REBUT] DivEye year**: references.bib 中 DivEye 标注为 TMLR 2026（diveye2026 entry, line 206-212），arXiv ID 2509.18880 (Sep 2025 submission, 2026 publication)。R2 认为应为 2025，但 publication venue year 标注 2026 是合理的——投稿于 2025 但 published in TMLR 2026。两种标注方式都有合理性，建议作者与 TMLR 官方 publication date 对齐即可。非实质问题。\n\n**[AGREE] conclusion.tex 命名混淆**: 文件名为 conclusion.tex 但实际包含的是 Limitations + Ethics Statement，而 Discussion and Conclusion 的内容在 discussion.tex 中。这确实容易让读者（或 collaborator）混淆。\n\n---\n\n### 总结\n\n我与 R2 的 9 条 Must-Fix/Should-Fix 中有 **9/9 方向一致**，但在严重程度上有 2 处分歧：\n1. R2-N2 我认为应从 critical 降为 should-fix（增量贡献存在，只是 positioning 需调整）\n2. R2-N3 我认为应从 critical 降为 should-fix（写作问题非方法学缺陷）\n\n我的独立审稿中标记的几个问题 R2 未覆盖：\n- 主表格 (Table 2, 5) 缺 std/CI（我的 R1-N2 续，R2 未提及）\n- LoRA rank sensitivity 缺失（我的 R1-W3 续，R2 未提及）\n- Accumulation 场景未验证（我的 R7-W3 续，R2 未提及）\n- Matched-quality control 缺失（我的 R7-W4 续，R2 未提及）\n\n这些方法学问题可能比 R2 关注的 framing/positioning 问题对接收决定影响更大。",
  "reviewer_id": "1",
  "project": "recursive_gen_depth_est"
}

_source line 76 · 2026-05-17T08:46:29.881Z_

[Round 1 审稿请求]:
## Review Assignment: R1 — Method & Experiments

**Venue:** EMNLP 2026 Main (via ACL Rolling Review)
**Paper:** "Ordinal Depth Estimation for Recursive Language Model Generations: A Depth Degradation Atlas Across Scales and Strategies"

**Scoring system (ARR 2025+):** Three scores on 1–5 scale: Soundness, Excitement, Overall Assessment.
- 5 = Top ~2%, exceptional
- 4 = Strong, warrants acceptance at a top *ACL venue
- 3 = Good, could be published but may not be top-tier
- 2 = Borderline, has merit but significant flaws
- 1 = Poor, significant flaws

**Your focus:** Method soundness, experimental rigor, statistical validity, reproducibility.

### Paper Summary
The paper formalizes "ordinal generation depth estimation": predicting how many recursive generation cycles (d ∈ {0,1,2,3}) a text passage has undergone, where depth 0 is human-written. The authors:
1. Construct a 3-model × 3-strategy × 4-depth benchmark (Pythia-1.4B, OLMo-1B, GPT-2 XL; nucleus/temperature/top-k; LoRA fine-tuning at each depth)
2. Extract 19 statistical features from a fixed reference model (Pythia-1.4B) and train RF/LR classifiers achieving 70–85% four-class accuracy (chance=25%)
3. Build a "Depth Degradation Atlas" revealing: universal depth markers (p90_ppl, var_surprisal), monotonic AUC decay, and strategy-dependent feature trajectory reversal under temperature sampling
4. Extend to 14B scale (Qwen2.5-14B, FP16, 76.2% accuracy) and prompted generation (92.8% retention vs BOS)
5. Propose a two-stage pipeline (strategy identification → conditioned depth classification) achieving 75.2%, +3.2pp over naive pooling
6. Apply classifiers to wild web data (RedPajama-v2, Dolma) showing consistent FPR gradients and temporal signal

### Evaluate These Dimensions

**A. Task Formalization (Section 3.1)**
- Is the problem definition (Eq. 1-2) clean and well-motivated?
- Is the EDD metric (Eq. 2) a meaningful contribution?

**B. Benchmark Construction (Section 3.2)**
- Is the 3×3×4 design sufficient? What's missing?
- Is LoRA-only fine-tuning a significant limitation?
- Sample sizes per configuration — are they reported clearly?

**C. Feature Extraction (Section 3.3)**
- Are 19 features well-chosen? Any obvious omissions?
- Is the fixed reference model (Pythia-1.4B) design sound?
- Is there a circularity concern when classifying Pythia-generated text?

**D. Classification (Section 3.4)**
- RF + LR only — should neural baselines be included?
- Is 5-fold stratified CV appropriate? Should there be held-out test sets?
- Are confidence intervals and significance tests adequate?

**E. Scale & Ecological Validity (Sections 5.2-5.3)**
- Does one 14B experiment suffice for "scale robustness" claims?
- Is the prompted generation experiment convincing for ecological validity?
- KS test for feature distribution comparison — appropriate?

**F. Two-Stage Pipeline (Section 5.4)**
- Is the v1→v2 improvement well-motivated?
- Oracle vs predicted gap analysis — is this analysis sound?
- Is 75.2% vs 72.0% (+3.2pp) a meaningful improvement?

**G. Wild-Data Calibration (Section 5.5)**
- Without ground-truth, how interpretable are these results?
- Is the temporal analysis (pre/post 2023) well-caveated?
- FPR estimation methodology — is it justified?

**H. Reproducibility**
- Are all hyperparameters specified?
- Is the experimental pipeline reproducible?
- Is code/data availability mentioned?

Provide: (1) numbered strengths, (2) numbered weaknesses, (3) questions for authors, (4) preliminary scores (Soundness, Excitement, Overall) with justification.
